@@4211991 <p> THE world 's association of camel scientists fought back angrily over Australian plans to kill wild dromedaries on the grounds that their flatulence adds to global warming . <p> The idea is " false and stupid ... a scientific aberration " , the International Society of Camelid Research and Development ( ISOCARD ) charged today , saying camels were being made culprits for a man-made problem . <p> " We believe that the good-hearted people and innovating nation of Australia can come up with better and smarter solutions than eradicating camels in inhumane ways , " it said . <p> The kill-a-camel suggestion is floated in a paper distributed by Australia 's Department of Climate Change and Energy Efficiency , as part of consultations for reducing the country 's carbon footprint . <p> The scheme is the brainchild of an Adelaide-based commercial company , Northwest Carbon , a land and animal management consultancy , which proposes whacking feral camels in exchange for carbon credits . <p> Camels were introduced to the Outback in the 19th century to help early settlers cope with hot , arid conditions . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ some , are a pest because of the damage they inflict to vegetation and their intestinal gases . <p> Each camel , according to the champions of a cull , emits 45kg of methane , the equivalent of one tonne a year in carbon dioxide ( CO2 ) , the main warming gas . <p> Northwest Carbon says it would shoot the camels from helicopters or corral them before sending them to an abattoir for eating by humans or pets . <p> But ISOCARD , an association of more than 300 researchers headquartered at al Ain University in the United Arab Emirates ( UAE ) , said the calculations were absurd . <p> " The estimation of methane emission by camels is based on cattle data extrapolation , " it said in a press release . <p> " The metabolic efficiency of camel is higher than that of cattle , ( ... ) camels are able to produce 20 per cent more milk by eating 20 per cent less food , they have different digestive system and are more efficient in the utilization of poor quality roughages , " it noted @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ intestines means their digestion is closer to that of monogastric animals , such as pigs , rather than as cattle and sheep , said ISOCARD . <p> " Therefore , the estimation of camel methane emission is quite debatable , as well as the estimated feral population . " <p> The 28 million camels in the world represent less than one per cent of all vegetation-eating biomass , and their emissions are just a tiny fraction of those made by cattle , it argued . <p> " The feral dromedary camels should be seen as an incomparable resource in arid environments , " the group said . " They can and should be exploited for food ( meat and milk ) , skin and hides , tourism etcetera . " <p> Australia is heavily reliant on coal-fired power and mining exports and has one of the highest per-capita carbon levels in the world . <p> The government plans to tax the nation 's 1,000 biggest polluters for carbon emissions from mid-2012 , with a fixed price giving way to a cap-and-trade scheme within five years . <p> To offset their emissions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ greenhouse gases that are avoided through other schemes . <p> News.com.au 's Privacy Policy includes important information about our collection , use and disclosure of your personal information ( including to provide you with targeted content and advertising based on your online activities ) . @@4213829 <h> Sketching an icon of refugee resilience <p> 11 CommentsVacy Vlazna 05 July 2011 <p> Handala is the eternal child ; the eternal 10-year-old refugee child conceived in the fragmented childhood of the late Palestinian cartoonist Naji Al-Ali . <p> In March , I first saw Handala in a painting in the wretched Bourj al Barajneh Palestinian refugee camp in Beirut ; a camp similar to Shatila Camp , where Al-Ali grew up after his family was dispossessed of their home in Al-Shajara village near Nazareth during the 1947 Nakba . <p> The Nakba , with its symbol of precious keys from stolen or demolished homes ( representing the right of return ) , is the Palestinian Catastrophe in which 750,000 Palestinians were forcefully expelled from their homes , from their beloved ancestral lands and roots by Israeli militia . <p> In a cartoon , Handala stands under a key hanging around the neck of the crucified Christ . Below is the caption ' Jesus is a Palestinian , says Naji Al-Ali ; like all Palestinian people he too dreams of returning to his home in Bethlehem . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' I drew him as a child who is not beautiful ; his hair is like the hair of a hedgehog who uses his thorns as a weapon . Handala is not a fat , happy , relaxed , or pampered child . He is barefooted like the refugee camp children , and he is an icon that protects me from making mistakes . Even though he is rough , he smells of amber . ' <p> I learnt about Handala while perusing A Child in Palestine : The cartoons of Naji Al-Ali in a Jerusalem bookstore . On the cover Handala stands beside what seems to be a candle taller than him , but is in fact a pen : Al-Ali 's pen was such a tour de force for truth that he was assassinated in London in 1987 . <p> The image of this little child reminded me of Michael Leunig 's iconic duck and his celebration of innocence as the ground of our conscience or what he calls ' the morality of the heart ' . <p> Handala stands with his little hands clasped behind him silently witnessing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ by Israeli state violence , by Arab and international betrayal and by callous human indifference . As a child he is vulnerable and needs protecting , so to stand by him we must reclaim , with heartfelt honesty , our lost innocence , to imagine what 10-year-old eyes , minds and hearts see and feel when suffering . <p> In Palestine , Handala is loved and cherished as a symbol of righteous steadfast resistance . <p> He is everywhere , not only on walls , stickers and T-shirts , but in the eyes of children as they watch their parents humiliated at checkpoints by abrasive Israeli soldiers ; in the eyes of children dying of cancer because medicines are blocked from entering Gaza ; in a child 's scream as her home is obliterated by Israeli missiles or bulldozers ; in the fear of 700 children per annum facing judges in Israeli military courts ; in the anxiety of children who are spat at and harassed by settlers and soldiers while on their way to and from school ; in the sound of sniper bullets fired at children near the Buffer @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ families . <p> ' I presented him to the poor and named him Handala as a symbol of bitterness , ' said Al-Ali . ' At first , he was a Palestinian child , but his consciousness developed to have a national and then a global and human horizon . He is a simple yet tough child , and this is why people adopted him and felt that he represents their consciousness . ' <p> Handala is the refugee child in detention suspended in an emotional prison barely hanging on to the universal right to protection . Handala is in the eyes of little Seenan watching his parents drown near the illusory safety of Christmas Island . He is the Aboriginal child belittled by Canberra 's intervention . <p> Humankind needs to be led by Handala to a life of conscience , compassion and responsibility.Dr Vacy Vlazna is Coordinator of Justice for Palestine Matters . She visited Palestine and Beirut earlier this year with APHEDA ( Union Aid Abroad ) . She was Human Rights advisor to the GAM team during during the second round of the Acheh peace talks in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Timor Justice Lobby as well as serving in the United Nations in East Timor for two years .. <h> Vacy Vlazna <h> Comments <p> Comments should be short , respectful and on topic . @@4215740 <h> The baseless campaign against GM food <p> Public statements this week by the head of Greenpeace International and his opinion piece on The Drum website confirm the activist group would rather court the media than protect the public interest . <p> The consequence of this media-driven approach is that Greenpeace agitates on issues they can easily sensationalise instead of those that are most important . Greenpeace 's determination to grab a headline regardless of the facts is most clearly shown by their baseless campaign against GM food . <p> It 's a little ironic that Kumi Naidoo , the head of Greenpeace International , is in Canberra this week to talk about climate action but at the same time Greenpeace commences a campaign against crop biotechnology and he submits a ludicrous and false article in support of that campaign . While he will acknowledge the primacy of the science on climate change , Mr Naidoo will conveniently ignore another substantial body of scientific evidence on GM crops because it does n't accord with his media strategy . He will continue to deny this evidence because it undermines @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ironic that those that demand governments listen , adhere to and take action on the mainstream science of climate change are also those who demand that government ignores the same mainstream , proven and peer-tested science on biotechnology in crops . <p> The inconvenient truth for Greenpeace is that GM crops are safe , a crucial agricultural tool in addressing global food security , and farmers want to use them . In addition , it 's a Greenpeace-generated fallacy that consumers do n't want GM food . <p> GM foods have been approved for sale and eaten safely in Australia , and around the world , for over 15 years . Over 1 trillion GM crop meals have been consumed over the past decade without a single health or safety issue . <p> GM crops have been more extensively studied and assessed for safety than any other foods and have been found to be safe . Independent scientific bodies such as the Australian Academy of Science and the World Health Organisation have confirmed the safety of foods produced from GM crops . <p> The Academy of Science found that " GM @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ consumed without any substantiated evidence of ill effects on health , and their safety confirmed by many peer-reviewed studies world-wide " . <p> Similarly , the World Health Organisation said " GM foods currently available on the international market have undergone risk assessments and are not likely to present risks for human health any more than their conventional counterparts " . <p> These assessments ensure the safety of any GM crop that is planted or consumed in Australia . It could be argued that the higher level of scrutiny that is applied to GM crops and foods makes them safer than conventionally produced foods , which undergo no equivalent assessment . <p> The richest irony in Mr Naidoo 's denunciation of GM food is that crop biotechnology has been recognised as one of the innovative agricultural tools that will help farmers cope with challenges of climate change while responding to the world 's ever-growing need for food . <p> Food security is a daunting task even without Greenpeace 's unilateral rejection of a key food production tool . The UN Food and Agriculture Organisation ( FAO ) says that global farm @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ nearly 100 per cent jump in developing countries , to feed the world in 2050 . At that time , the population will have risen to about 9.2 billion compared to 6.9 billion in 2010 . The total number of undernourished people in 2010 was estimated at 925 million , higher that it was 40 years ago . <p> The magnitude of the food security challenge was reinforced earlier this year by a study on global food and farming futures conducted by the UK government 's chief scientific advisor and the Foresight Panel . The project involved around 400 leading experts and stakeholders from about 35 countries across the world , and drew upon over 100 peer-reviewed papers . It concluded that the only way to meet future food needs is through sustainable intensification of agriculture , through the combined use of GM crops with conventional and organic growing methods . <p> The FAO has concurred , saying that a new approach based on sustainable intensification of crop output is necessary to allow farmers to produce more from the same area of land by raising yields and at the same time @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on the environment . <p> GM crops have delivered a wide range of benefits to farmers including reduced water , fuel and insecticide use , as well as increased farmer incomes across the globe by more than $50 billion . Greenpeace claims that GM crops increase costs to farmers and states there is no viable market for such crops . Both have proven to be wrong , with Australian farmers getting access to key international markets and choosing to plant GM crops over successive seasons . <p> Greenpeace claims that consumers do n't want GM food are comprehensively scuttled by a series of independent international and local consumer surveys showing that consumers around the world are happy to continue to eat GM food . <p> So Greenpeace , having lost the battle to deny Australian farmers the right to grow GM canola , are again crying wolf on GM wheat in the hope of getting a juicy headline . <p> Mr Naidoo 's article , as with Greenpeace 's previous reports on this issue , contains absolutely no new evidence to suggest that GM wheat is unsafe and the few studies @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ or thoroughly discredited by the scientific community . The claim that conventional breeding is safer than genetic modification is not the view conveyed by scientific experts in Australia and abroad . <p> The tenor of Kumi Naidoo 's rhetoric suggests that he knows Greenpeace 's anti-GM campaign is on shaky ground . The resort to demonisation of crop biotechnology developers smacks of desperation . As with most public debate , it is those that resort to baseless alarmist scaremongering that should be assessed closely by the public and approached with a high degree of scepticism . <p> Both private companies and government funded research organisations are trialing GM crops in Australia . The companies are developing GM crops because they want new and innovative products to sell to their customers which is demand driven . The governments are involved because they see an overwhelming public good being delivered by some of the technologies . <p> In light of this , Mr Naidoo 's accusation that four companies will own the world 's food chain is akin to the most hysterical of climate denialists warning against the one world government - it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Why Greenpeace continues to raise concern over GM crops is perplexing . It is particularly baffling because the world faces huge challenges in feeding a growing population in a changing climate while continuing to protect and conserve natural resources and the environment . True environmentalists should be supporting new crop biotechnologies . Those genuinely committed to addressing world hunger should be embracing GM crops . <p> In persisting with their quest to secure headlines without facts , Greenpeace has demonstrated yet again that they 're the worst kind of political agitators and not credible contributors to the public debate . 
@@4215749 <h> Media-driven decision making <p> Last night the Federal Government announced that it is lifting the ban on live cattle exports to Indonesia . The ban was put in place a month ago after the ABC broadcasted footage showing animal cruelty in some Indonesian abattoirs . <p> This controversial ban and the drafting of legislation regarding face coverings demonstrate the unfortunate situation of politicians acquiescing to aggressive media campaigns . <p> Media companies are not supposed to operate as public relations or lobbying firms . Rather , they should be focussing on the business of getting and reporting the news . Recently , however , the lines have been blurred with some media organisations playing a more active role in campaign journalism advocating strongly in support of issues ranging from climate change to gambling . <p> These reporters will argue that they are merely doing their job of exposing incompetence , policy violations and the like . This may be true when reporting is factual and balanced , but certainly not when it is biased and unrepresentative . An Essential Media survey conducted in May found that 69 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ all sides of a story and 54 per cent disagreed that the media reported the news accurately . <p> In the case of Australian cattle being treated cruelly , why has it taken until Tuesday , more than five weeks later , for anyone in politics or the media to question the accuracy of the ABC Four Corners report ? What did Animals Australia do with the footage for more than five months ? <p> Well crafted stories are just that -- a snapshot of a particular issue as seen through the pen or lens of an individual editor or producer with a particular agenda . These stories must be independently verified in order to ascertain the true scope and extent of a problem . <p> Which federal government official was given the responsibility of verifying the claims made in the ABC report ? We can only speculate on the decision-making process employed by the Government to arrive at its decision of banning all livestock export . <p> With the media vigorously championing the case for animal welfare , a decision was made after only nine days of the program going @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to assess the veracity of the claims and to determine the best possible solution to resolve the problem . The Government has failed to explain why it did not stop at the initial ban to affected abattoirs only . Perhaps the media pressure had something to do with the extension to a complete ban . <p> When the full impact of the rushed decision became apparent , the same media then ironically changed and went into bat for the cattle farmers . The ban has already cost the Government at least $30 million in damages and is likely to be far greater in the long run . <p> Now to the furore and frenzy surrounding the issue of police identifying people wearing face coverings . <p> The New South Wales Government plans to introduce legislation into State Parliament next month which would close a supposed loophole regarding face coverings . Apparently , people may refuse to show their faces when requested to do so by police during routine road stops . <p> This decision again was forced by an intense media campaign . One particular media organisation has gone feral with @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not one but sometimes multiple stories a day on the issue -- most featuring prominently on both its front and home pages . It has gone to the extent of paying for legal assistance to force the court to release information about the case . <p> Strangely , after two weeks of sustained coverage the same media company reported the false claim that the laws being drafted follow the furore over a women 's refusal to remove her face veil when pulled over by police . <p> Anyone who has viewed the footage knows that the face veil was not an issue for the police officer during the road stop . In fact , she lifted her veil so that the officer could verify her identity against the driver 's license provided . <p> All the exaggerated media focus on the removal of face coverings for identification purposes is therefore disingenuous and supports the charge that campaign journalism is at play . <p> Amazingly the rush to introduce new legislation is happening while the judge in the case concerned is yet to release his written judgment . The current debate and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reports of the court 's ruling . <p> The initial coverage clearly stated that the judge could not be certain that the person who signed the declaration was the same person pulled over . The case hinged primarily on discrepancies in signatures and not on facial identity . <p> Voter confidence in elected officials is dented when poor decision making processes driven by aggressive media campaigns become the norm . Readers will also preference responsible media over lobbying campaigns disguised as news reports . 
@@4215777 <h> Some pepper for Bernard Salt : A Generation Y response to writing and saving the planet <p> Sophie Cunningham 's recent article ' Time to hear , read , review and award the words of women ' got me thinking , why are female writers overlooked , and more specifically , why are young , up-and-coming female writers largely disregarded ? This concern was partly born out of my own experiences as a 23-year old Generation Y female when my counter-responses to highly topical pieces in the Murdoch press were n't published . <p> The media is always encouraging younger generations to make a difference by getting involved , yet when they do often such efforts are still discounted . There seems to be two main reasons for the current age and gender inequalities within the publishing environment : the focus on commercialism and the author 's social position within society . The recent commercial success and celebrity recognition of journalist , writer and KPMG Partner , Bernard Salt 's latest book The Big Tilt is a case in point . <p> In The Australian , Salt @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ n't reach an old dog with nude pics ' where he expressed mixed feelings about ' Weinergate ' . This term describes the plight of U.S. Democrat Congressman Anthony Weiner ( now resigned ) who was caught sending photographs of his ... well , ' weiner ' to various women . It seems that some U.S. columnists took a concept from Salt 's recent book The Big Tilt , that of ' hotness delusion syndrome ' , and applied it to the case of Weiner . There are 15 per cent more single women than men at the age of 44 . This , Salt says , leads men of that age to delusions of grandeur , thinking that they are ' hotter ' ( i.e. physically more attractive ) than they really are . <p> Advertisement <p> I read the passage in The Big Tilt describing ' hotness delusion syndrome ' . Assuming that Salt is presenting a text based on scientific research , rather than imaginative prose , I examined the book to find peer-reviewed psychological papers on this alleged ' syndrome ' . I found no such works @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and a selected bibliography of only two pages of 20 books , five of which are authored by Salt . <p> No scientific evidence is presented by Salt of the existence of the phenomenon of ' hotness delusion syndrome ' other than research conducted by him . We simply do n't know if men in his age group do believe what Salt says they believe . It is supposition , pure and simple . Yet even if it were somehow true , the feminist in me questions why a woman would need to be validated by a relationship with a man anyway . Seminal Australian feminists such as Germaine Greer and Eva Cox have shown that you do not need a man to feel complete : there is nothing wrong with being single in your forties . <p> Interestingly , throughout ' Weinergate ' there has been little discussion of Anthony Weiner 's wife Huma Abedin , a strong and courageous woman battering against the media storm . It is certainly noteworthy that she has not received any commissions from the various public figures making a quick buck out of her @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ if it means celebrity mentions and publishing success . Meanwhile , scholarly works with ' boring ' subject matter but more appropriate content like population growth and peak oil are largely ignored . <p> Salt is described as a ' demographer ' in The Australian and some journalists have described him as a ' leading demographer ' . As far as I have been able to ascertain Salt does not have a PhD in demography , a specialised social science , nor does he have an extensive publication record of peer-reviewed scholarly articles in leading international demographic journals making him a peer of say Professor Graeme Hugo at the University of Adelaide or Dr. Bob Birrell of Monash University . This is not to discard Salt 's work . It does mean that we should be particularly careful to seek evidential backing for all of The Big Tilt 's assertions . <p> Salt is well known for his articles in The Australian defending ' Big Australia ' which he defines as 36 million people by 2051 ( 180,000 annual net overseas migration ) against a smaller Australia of 29 million ( @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ modeling by Monash University 's Centre for Population , on the assumption that migration levels , fertility and life expectancy continue at the 2010 rate , yield a figure of 42 million by 2050 . <p> According to Salt , large-scale immigration is necessary for two reasons . The first is to ameliorate the ageing of the population . This argument is repeated in the book , but with no scientific proof . There are no references to modeling and no first-hand modeling conducted by Salt . The consensus in demography is that immigration does little to offset demographic ageing , primarily because immigration and fertility do not have equivalent impacts on the population structure . According to the Australian Bureau of Statistics with a net overseas migration of 50,000 per year , the median age of the population in 2051 would be 47.2 years and with a net overseas migration of 150,000 per year , the median age only falls 2.6 years to 44.6 years . Immigration , contrary to Salt , does not solve the problem of demographic ageing . <p> Advertisement <p> Salt 's other argument for ' @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ does not have an adequate tax base to ' fund the retirement of baby boomers who dutifully paid tax throughout their working lives in the expectation that they would receive sufficient funding to live their later years with dignity ' ( p. 20 ) . With this we come to the crunch of things : we need a big tax base to continue to give the baby boomer generation a good life . Writing from the perspective of a 23-year old Generation Y female , who like most people my age group , face uncertainty in employment , I am not sympathetic . Baby boomers lived through the good times and should by retirement have enough superannuation to live in dignity . No doubt , most of the ' me generation ' will continue to cling to their jobs even after they pass age 65 . <p> From a social scientific perspective , Salt does not substantiate his tax base argument . He neither shows that a population of 29 million is inadequate , or that a population of 36 million is adequate . That of course relates just to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ecological costs of a larger population . There are no cost/benefit analyses conducted . <p> As a sample of Salt 's reasoning consider his conclusion reached about the high U.N . projection of a world population of 36 billion at the end of the 22nd century , one of a number of population projection scenarios . Salt says : ' It 's fair to say the Australian continent is most likely to be commandeered , and not necessarily by our foes . But this outlook is unlikely to eventuate because so great will be the global pressure on resources that the four horsemen of the apocalypse will most surely intervene -- war , pestilence , disease and famine -- to bring humanity back into line ' ( p. 272 ) . In this passage , Salt is departing from the Julian Simon/Matt Ridley techno-economic optimism that sees no limits to growth and celebrates human population expansion because more people mean more problem-solvers . <p> Dick Smith 's Population Crisis puts the case that ecological limits are much lower than 36 billion and in fact , there is evidence that the world @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Smith is no more within my demographic than Bernard Salt : he is also a businessman , not a professionally trained scientist . However unlike Salt , Smith endeavours to support his view with six pages of footnoted references to scholarly and scientific works . <p> My position on the population issue is one of agreement with Dick Smith and I have detailed this in my online book Sleepwalking to Catastrophe . It may not have the same potential for commercial success as ' hotness delusion syndrome ' but by advocating environmental change to provide for future generations , at least it has monumental importance . Top that , Bernard Salt . The subtitle of The Big Tilt is What Happens When the Boomers Bust and the Xers and Ys Inherit the Earth . @@4217695 <h> Cucumbers and climate change deniers <p> 9 CommentsBrian Matthews 07 July 2011 <p> Francisco Sosa Wagner , a Spanish member of the European Parliament , enjoys a modest fame as a doctor of law , university professor , historian , and prize-winning writer . His columns on European political , legal and other topics appear regularly in the Spanish press , and he was awarded the Medal of the Order of Saint Raymond of Pe ? afort for his outstanding contributions to the field of justice and the law . <p> His European Parliament profile shows him to be a conscientious attendee of the plenary sessions ( 103 out of a possible 109 days ) ; he has asked 28 parliamentary questions , delivered 15 plenary speeches , contributed to the amendment of 13 reports and given two opinions . <p> A fit-looking 65 , stylishly bow-tied and coiffed , dignified but with the trace of an impish smile , Wagner might not bestride the European Parliament like a colossus , but he is clearly not to be taken lightly or in any way disrespectfully . All @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ him initially puzzling and certainly surprising . <p> Wearing one of his trademark colourful bow ties , Francisco Sosa Wagner , MEP , is standing at his parliamentary desk holding at head height a long green cucumber . His message : ' We need to restore the honour of the cucumber ! ' <p> It may not have been one of his more telling or resonant speeches -- though it was surely his briefest -- but it was topical because the cucumber had , of course , been grievously besmirched when identified -- wrongly -- as the cause of the recent deadly E.coli outbreak in Germany . <p> It 's fairly difficult to look dignified and serious while brandishing a cucumber . Cucumbers are themselves vaguely comic and are central to innumerable phallic jokes and claims . Waving one of these priapic evocations above your head is bound to have a deflating and distracting effect on your spoken message no matter how passionate , powerful and apposite your words might be . <p> But it is a measure of Wagner 's dedication to the cause that he was prepared to risk @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ motives and his imagery , to rescue the innocent vegetable . Anyway , it worked . His dramatic fist-full of penile cucumis sativus prompted further investigations which exonerated the inoffensive cowcumber , as it was once called , and pointed the finger at fenugreek sprouts imported from Egypt . <p> The Spanish MEP 's willingness to risk his dignity and credibility in an apparently eccentric cause reminded me of Vyacheslav Ilyin , whose story I came across in a dentist waiting room copy of New Scientist a few years ago . <p> Vyacheslav Ilyin is pretty much like you and me , allowing for some cultural differences . He turns an honest rouble at Moscow 's Institute for Biological and Medical Problems , trails to and from his office in all weathers protected by his ushanka ( one of those Russian fur hats ) , loves his family , despairs at the indifferent form of his team , Lokomotiv Moscow , and would n't give you a bent kopeck for politicians . <p> According to the article , Vyacheslav 's government-backed and classified research interest is how the astronauts in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ seems that , after the small matter of ensuring that a spacecraft actually stays in space and sticks to the game plan , the biggest problem in orbit is waste disposal . As a result , Vyacheslav Ilyin is obsessed with underpants . It turns out that astronauts must wear the same underpants for up to a week at a time . In short , the biggest problem up there is down there . <p> At the drop of a ushanka , Vyacheslav will tell you that each astronaut generates about 2.5 kg of uncompressed waste every day . Lateral thinker that he is , Vyacheslav scorns such remedies as squeezing the spacecraft full of whitegoods . What he 's working on is a mixture of bacteria that will digest the astronauts ' underpants and in the process produce methane to power the space craft . <p> Astronautical underpants are Vyacheslav Ilyin 's equivalent of Francisco Sosa Wagner 's phallic cucumber . Both are difficult propositions to promote with dignity but both men had the passion and the courage to do so publicly and unflinchingly and both made a significant contribution @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ variously referred to as Lord and Viscount , who has called Ross Garnaut a fascist ( for which he was forced to apologise ) , has delivered a speech in front of a large Nazi swastika , compares climate change consensus to Nazi eugenics and who glibly wondered what has happened to the Australian ' fair go ' -- a concept so dear to English Viscounts and Lords -- when a succession of venues cancelled his bookings . <p> There are easily enough crackpots around -- believing everything from the moon landings to the twin towers attack is a ' put-up job ' -- to ensure that conspiracy theorists and professional deniers like Monckton will always get an audience . @@4219535 <h> Search smh : <h> Search in : <h> Outside the box <p> Art and Design <p> Date <p> July 8 , 2011 <p> ( 0 ) <h> Liza Power <p> They are changing the face of Melbourne . From the upcycling of discarded classrooms , to communal work spaces that foster problem solving and decorative walls that urge you to touch them , a new breed of zealous twentysomethings and thirtysomethings are leaving their mark . Meet the city 's most promising young architects . <p> OUR environment has a profound effect on our moods , our capacity to think creatively and our willingness to connect with others . Classrooms designed to accommodate different learning models produce better educational outcomes . Hospitals that weave nature into their architecture foster faster recovery . <p> Offices conceived to facilitate flexible work patterns stimulate productivity . Their motto : research , dialogue and action . After a stint at a commercial practice , she and partner Ammon Beyerle formed Here Studio , based on principles of activist architecture . <p> Their project at Donkey Wheel House involved converting the third floor of the heritage-listed Bourke Street building into offices for Hub , an organisation spanning 26 cities in five continents to promote social change . Their first step involved assembling a group of eight cross-disciplinary practitioners and inviting them to " occupy " the space . " Treat it like a new relationship , play around , flirt and try things out in it , " says James , who held workshops and rehearsals for her WELOVEPT choir to map the acoustics . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ her thinking is a theory of ecologies : " What makes this space interesting , the community of co-workers who 'll inhabit it unique and how can we use a space to encourage productive connections between them ? " <p> Prototypes of tables , chairs and partitions were made , trialled and placed on wheels to allow flexible configurations of space . Visually permeable partitions were made to section off spaces , lights constructed from recycled bicycle spokes wrapped in cardboard and extension cords bound in crochet ( heritage restrictions prevented the building of false walls to conceal them ) . Hand-knitted chair covers lend a welcoming , home-made aesthetic . Lavish velvet theatre curtains were hung to section off work zones and absorb sound and a wall designated for staff members to draw or create murals . Indoor plants adorned with tags denoting species and watering requirements were arranged to create leafy sanctuaries . <p> On any day , up to 60 people ( graphic designers , IT and social networking professionals , community organisation workers ) might work at Hub . Some work all day , others for a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ The space is reconfigured regularly and staff move from desk to bean bag and couch , depending on their mood . For James , the start of good design is communication : " I 'm a person , so are you . If we talk , we find a solution together . " <p> Rebecca Naughtin . Photo : Simon Schluter <p> NEW THOUGHTS ON THE BLOCK <p> Sophie Dyring , 36 , Heathmont Park <p> When Dyring graduated from RMIT in 2001 , she won the Association of Women in Architecture Prize for outstanding female graduate . After a spell as a sole practitioner , she joined Graeme Gunn , this year 's Australian Institute of Architects Gold Medallist , to form GunnDyring . <p> Jefa Greenaway . Photo : Simon Schluter <p> For Dyring , smart architecture begins with passive design principles : face buildings north , shade the west side , emphasise cross ventilation and so on . They 're rules architects learn from day one but in many developments , where buyers select residences to fit block sizes -- House A fits on a 14-metre-wide block -- @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Heathmont reimagines the former site of Heathmont Technical College as a residential development . But rather than " cookie cut " the 3.6-hectare site into standard blocks , she 's revived Gunn 's work on cluster houses from the 1970s to produce a development of 66 residences . Her priorities : affordable , higher-density living that does n't compromise residents ' quality of life . The result : eight house types designed according to passive design principles to accommodate families , couples and empty nesters , aggregated open spaces and links to the surrounding community . Materials reference the surrounding streetscape . <p> The challenges were many : the suburban quarter-acre block remains a dream for most Australians , while the project asks its occupants to abandon the 1.8-metre paling fence in favour of shared green areas and visually permeable barriers . Real estate agents need to be educated about resale values and investment returns , data Dyring has collated from Gunn 's past projects . <p> Eugene Cheah . Photo : Simon Schluter <p> " People who buy into them will be buying into both a house and a philosophy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ with exponential population growth and climate change , we must find smarter ways to live . <p> Still , she says a recent Grattan report demonstrates people will forgo the suburban dream when they 're assured proximity to schools , public transport and shopping . " This collective idea that we all want this detached house on a block is slowly changing , " she says . <p> For Dyring , architecture is about shifting people 's ways of thinking in baby steps . <p> Mike Sharp . Photo : Simon Schluter <p> TOOLS OF THE TRADE <p> Rebecca Naughtin , 31 , Ethiopian Congregational Hall <p> Naughtin 's office on Queens Road takes in views of Albert Park Lake , St Kilda , the bay -- and on a clear day the You Yangs . The same breadth of perspective underpins her work , which spans pro bono , hospitality , residential , commercial projects and feasibility studies , often at the same time . <p> Like Dyring , Naughtin paired with an established practitioner , Bruce Allen , to form Allen Naughtin . The two work from the same @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ its own toolbox of analysis , approach and problem-solving skills . " <p> She describes the Ethiopian Congregational Hall as a " love job " , one of a string of projects the practice has undertaken on behalf of the Uniting Church in the western suburbs . It followed from work on a Sudanese refugee education centre , which involved transforming a community hall into computer classrooms . <p> The Ethiopian project is not about money , it 's about making architecture more accessible to community members who need it most , she says . The congregation approached the practice with plans to place three shipping containers on an industrial site in Sunshine : one for congregations , the other two storage . Naughtin 's task lay in persuasion : " Shipping containers are expensive and you 're not going to meet building regulations unless you spend a fortune bringing them up to spec . " Given the current surplus of old school portables , orphans of Labor 's education revolution , she sourced them instead , factoring in planning requirements such as toilets , openable windows and parking . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ role of project manager , hiring community tradespeople . Which is not to say she 'll have the last word on interior design . " We had a Sudanese tiler on the last project , very proud of himself , " she says . " He 'd got these pale grey tiles , a great deal of them , and decided to match them with electric-blue grout . He said : ' I thought it needed a lift . " ' Unorthodox approaches aside , at the heart of the project is Naughtin 's philosophy of what architecture should do : " Architects need to be accessible and find ways to showcase their skills and expertise to the general public , while people need to understand that even the smallest , simplest design decisions can have a huge impact on their quality of life . " <p> STRUCTURAL GYMNASTICS <p> Jefa Greenaway , 40 , bespoke Armadale residence <p> When Greenaway graduated from the University of Melbourne , he became the first indigenous registered architect in Victoria . <p> He 's since collected multiple awards , including the 1999 RAIA Presidents @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a place on the Dulux Study Tour , designed to give Australia 's best emerging architects a backstage pass to Europe 's celebrated architectural sights . <p> Along with lecturing at Melbourne University , Greenaway runs his own practice and works at SJB , where he recently designed a bespoke residence for a client looking to build a family home . <p> He admits it was a dream project ; the client cited his modernist inspirations and provided a sizeable budget to work with . His challenge lay in taking design principles conceived by the likes of Mies van de Rohe 80-odd years ago and finding engineers who could master the structural gymnastics behind the clean lines and slim planes of his design . <p> Playing on the architectural lineage of modernism and " building on layers of history " , he delights in " returning to the essence of craft , of clarity , simplicity " . Designing a family home is , he says , the most intimate of projects . <p> " Homes have evolved over time , " he says . " Whereas in the past the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ now it 's the island bench . " <p> The beauty of the bespoke home is that it 's customised to the nth degree , he says . Even if the pragmatics of bedrooms and bathrooms are straightforward , less so is determining what people want from individual spaces or " how they want them to feel when they 're in them " . He says good design should engage the senses " on every level . A wall should be beautiful in a way that you 're tempted to touch it and materials should be used to create rhythms of texture and flow . " <p> The key material for the Armadale house is travertine , a pitted stone that interplays with light to produce plays of shadow . It 's combined with steel , timber and glass , which creates medleys of reflection throughout the house ; an approach he calls tactile modernism . The house 's ceilings appear like hovering planes , with the emphasis on blurring boundaries between inside and out . <p> In his teaching , Greenaway broaches notions of indigenous placemaking but he says the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ architecture has to nurture individual and collective senses of identity , are crucial to everyone , regardless of their heritage . <p> THE BEST MEDICINE <p> Eugene Cheah , 34 , Dandenong Hospital Emergency Department <p> Cheah cut his teeth in residential architecture before joining Bates Smart to try his hand at projects of greater scale , budget and impact . For him , " architecture should have a tangible effect on those who interact with it , " and there are few better spheres to exercise this than in key pieces of public infrastructure . <p> Emergency departments are n't places for bold , egotistical design flourishes ; they 're projects defined by rigid health , building and government regulations : bed widths , corridor breadths , ventilation , lighting and air-filtering systems are specified and not negotiable . For Cheah , the challenge lay in making the rule book elastic . <p> A key starting point was the history , demographic and geography of Dandenong , which , home to 156nationalities , is Victoria 's most culturally diverse city . To reconcile such diversity , an " extremely clear @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ removing the need for excess signage and alleviating the risk of people becoming lost or disoriented . " EDs emergency departments are designed to be fast-paced , efficient and clinical , " he says . Often situated in the guts or bowels of a hospital , they tend to be " contained , alien spaces devoid of natural light " . By contrast , Dandenong 's ED was fortuitously sited at the hospital 's front and part of Cheah 's brief was to offer the hospital a fresh face . <p> Given most patients associate emergency departments with high levels of anxiety , Cheah embraced a " human-centric approach " . A floorplan of clustered wards linked by corridors that led to large windows was drawn . Each cluster ( paediatrics , fast-track and so on ) was colour-coded ; the team used high ceilings and box structures to make wards identifiable for easy navigation . To harness natural light but avoid compromising the privacy and security of patients , windows were shielded in a gauze of vertical panels , which , varying in width and shape , dress the building @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the project employed a team of 12 architects , interior designers and dedicated health planners , each of whom worked from the macro to micro scale -- even the bespoke curtain fabric had to adhere to guidelines for patients with conditions such as epilepsy . While Cheah concedes some rigid briefs " tend to elicit very little innovation " , he argues the best outcomes stem from thinking not of " what 's left outside the prescribed specifications but of what can be worked into them " . <p> URBAN UPCYCLING <p> Mike Sharp , 30 , Cafe Acustico <p> Sharp 's first love is cabinet making ; his passion is designing things he can make himself . His fascination lies in foraging suburban demolition yards for recycled materials , storing them in friends ' sheds and then imagining them into new and wonderful inventions . <p> The winner of the 2010 Australian Institute of Architects Woodhead Graduate Prize in sustainable architecture , Sharp left his role at a commercial firm to found The Summer Office with partner Jess in 2010 . Sustainability is key to the pair 's approach @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ model of project management ensures every stage of construction is environmentally sound . <p> Cafe Acustico , in Union Street , Brunswick , will find its home in a near-derelict building that once served as a commercial printing business . At just 12square metres , it 's inspired as much by European espresso bars as the urban landscape that skirts it . Its walls will be tiled in wood cut from reclaimed railway sleepers ; the bar sits a stone 's throw from Jewell railway station and its customers will be commuters . Sink splashbacks will be cut from ornate , 1970s glass shower screens and along the building 's west-facing wall a series of window shutters will open to reveal a vertical herb garden . <p> The coffee grinds will be composted to feed the garden , with produce used in the bar or given to customers . Sharp 's idea was to give the building two lives -- graffiti clad , destitute at night ; green and resplendent during the day . Sharp 's holistic approach means design decisions remain flexible ; clients can watch stages unfold and feel @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ n't a gimmick ; his project is to make them " beautiful and viable building materials " . <p> Sharp delights in experimenting with materials and cites James Krenov as his greatest inspiration . The opening page of Krenov 's tome on woodwork cites a Ming Dynasty poem . " It describes men fasting and then going into the forest to find the perfect tree for the emperor , the tree telling the man what to make from it . @@4220563 <p> Australian households will be handed more than $15 billion in compensation to help offset the impact of the Government 's new carbon tax , Prime Minister Julia Gillard has confirmed . <p> Some 90 per cent of households will get tax cuts and/or extra payments when the $23 per tonne price kicks in on July 1 next year . <p> The Government says the package 's tax reforms and other compensation measures mean the average household will be 20 cents a week better off . <p> However households can expect to see consumer prices rise by an average of 0.7 per cent due to the effect of the carbon price on large emitters . <p> There will be extra payments for pensioners and families with children , while the tax reforms come with a guarantee that no-one will pay more income tax . <p> The Government says 4 million households will be better off after compensation , 6 million will be no worse off , and 8 million will receive partial compensation for the price rises . <p> Known as the Clean Energy Supplement , the Government @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ increase in pensions , allowances and family payments . <p> The scheme also includes $9.2 billion over three years for trade-exposed industry , with emissions-intensive industries such as aluminium , zinc and steel manufacturing getting more than 94 per cent of their carbon permits for free . <p> The scheme is projected to cut 159 million tonnes of carbon pollution from the atmosphere by 2020 - the equivalent of taking 45 million cars off the road . <p> The assistance measures to households include a rise in the tax-free threshold from $6,000 to $18,000 , and up to $338 per year extra for single pensioners . <p> All taxpayers with incomes below $80,000 will get tax cuts - most about $300 a year . <p> Treasurer Wayne Swan says average households will pay an extra $9.90 per week while average assistance will be $10.10 per week . <p> What it means for you : <p> Up to $338 extra per year for single pensioners and self-funded retirees , and up to $510 per year for pensioner couples combined . <p> Up to $110 per child for a family that receives Family @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ families that receive Family Tax Benefit Part B. <p> Up to $218 extra per year for single income support recipients and $390 per year for couples combined for people on allowances . <p> Up to $234 per year for single parents in addition to the increased family payments they receive . <p> As expected , around 500 highest polluting businessses will pay the carbon price - which will increase by 2.5 per cent above inflation until July 1 2015 when a market-based emissions trading scheme will kick in . <p> While fuel is exempt for individual motorists and small business , diesel for heavy vehicles like semi-trailers will be paying the carbon price from 2014 , after a two-year moratorium . <p> The scheme will be administered by a new Climate Change Authority ( CCA ) to be headed up by former Reserve Bank governor and Treasury Secretary , Bernie Fraser . <p> The CCA will recommend future pollution caps but the final decision will rest with the Government . <p> The main features of the plan are : <p> Initial carbon price of $23 per tonne of carbon pollution to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2.5 per cent in real terms . <p> A transition to a market-based emissions trading scheme in 2015 . <p> $9.2 billion from the revenue stream to help businesses and workers impacted by the plan . <p> Tax cuts and pension increases to protect people from higher prices . <p> A $1.2 billion Clean Technology Program to improve energy efficiency in manufacturing and to support research and development . <p> Australia 's most polluting electricity generators will be closed and replaced with gas-fired units by 2020 . <p> An Australian Renewable Energy Agency to manage a $10 billion Clean Energy Finance Corporation to fund new clean energy technology . <p> An Australian Renewable Energy Agency to manage a $3.2 billion clean energy budget . <p> A target of 20 per cent renewable energy by 2020 . <p> Agriculture excluded from paying the carbon price . <h> ' National transition ' <p> " The science is in , we know that our climate is changing , " Ms Gillard said as she unveiled the plans on Sunday . <p> " This has been a difficult debate that has brought us to this @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the chance to get this done . " <p> Ms Gillard says nine out of 10 households will receive compensation under the plan , either through tax cuts or extra payments . <p> " Carbon pricing is the foundation stone for this package ... it 's got a focus on energy efficiency , it 's got a focus on renewable energy , it 's got a focus on the land sector , " she said . <p> " This is a big day in Canberra ... as we transition our nation to a clean energy future . <p> " I want Australian families to understand what it will mean to them . I want them to understand we are seizing a clean energy future . <p> " Standing here as PM I understand that this has been a long , complicated and at some times very difficult debate for Australians to follow . <p> " We are now moving from the days of words to deeds .. we are going to get this done . We are going to create a clean energy future . <p> Mr Swan described the plan @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ca n't allow itself to become a " technological backwater " . <p> " We 've got to price carbon pollution to drive the investment in innovation and to provide the incentive for energy efficiency and to develop renewable energy , " he said . <p> " Failing to do so means that we would be passing on lower living standards to our children and to our grandchildren . " <h> Permits for industry <p> The assistance for high-polluting industries which rely on exporting their products will be through free permits , and will apply to aluminium smelting , steel manufacturing , flat glass making , zinc smelting and pulp and paper making . <p> There is a $300 million package to support jobs in steel manufacturing by encouraging innovation and another $1.3 billion towards rewarding coal mines that reduce their emissions . <p> The Government says the $23 carbon price will add about $1.80 per tonne of coal produced - but " gassy " mines will face " significant " cost pressures . <p> The companies that do not meet " emissions intensive , trade exposed " criteria will receive @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ equipment . <p> Small business - while not considered polluting - will benefit from an extended instant asset write-off threshold to $6,500 . <p> An Energy Security Fund will be established to pay for the closure of Australia 's most polluting electricity generators - believed to be Victoria 's brown coal generators . <p> The Government wants to remove 2,000 megawatts of capacity from these generators before 2020 and replace them with cleaner electricity generators - preferably gas . <p> The Greens were able to negotiate significant support for renewable energy programs including a $10 billion Clean Energy Finance Corporation which will provide loans for innovation when commercial banks are unwilling to fund projects . <p> Another $3.2 billion in existing funding will be brought under one agency to support new technologies from the research and development to market stages . <p> There is also a $40 million program for remote Indigenous communities for clean power supplies - like solar panels and wind turbines - so existing diesel generating plants can be replaced . <p> Nearly $1 billion will go towards a biodiversity fund to help farmers and community groups look @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 
@@4221452 <h> The virtuous circle of Gillard 's climate tax <p> 22 CommentsLin Hatfield Dodds 10 July 2011 <p> The Government and the Multi Party Climate Change Committee ( MPCCC ) have crafted a historic package of reforms : driving long-run reductions in carbon pollution , simplifying personal tax and making it fairer , and reducing poverty traps and barriers to work . <p> Each of these steps would be worthwhile on their own . Together , they send two clear signals . First , the Government has found its mojo . Second , minority government is not an impediment to good policy . <p> The package lays the groundwork for Australia 's transition to a clean energy future , allowing us to play our part in global action to address climate change . It will put a price on carbon , drive public and private investment in the development of a strong low carbon energy sector , and support energy efficiency . It adopts a more ambitious , and realistic , long-run emissions target . It also establishes the institutions required to oversee and fine tune policies to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reductions at home if global ambition moves closer to the goals called for by the climate science . <p> Crucially , the package ensures that low income Australians will not be left behind . Low income and vulnerable Australians will receive permanent increases in allowances , pensions and family benefits that more than cover average cost of living impacts . Initial upfront payments , followed by fortnightly payments , will greatly assist households on tight budgets to manage cost of living increases . <p> Payments to most households will be provided as an upfront lump sum before the carbon price takes effect in July 2012 , with fortnightly payments beginning in March or July 2013 for pensions , most allowances , and family payments , and in January 2014 for students on Youth Allowance . <p> The real value of these payments will be maintained through indexation . This protects households who have few options and are least able to cope with rising energy prices . There are also commitments to provide targeted supported for improved energy efficiency for low income households , with potential to address both the causes and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ disappointment is that the arrangements are based on a 1.7 per cent increase in existing payments , rather than basic energy needs . This means that a single pensioner will receive up to $80 more per year than someone on unemployment benefits . For a couple , the gap is $130 per year , despite evidence showing that people on Newstart often spend a higher share of their income on energy . <p> But the package goes beyond offsetting cost of living impacts . In addition to the increases in allowance and payments , the package provides significant reductions in personal tax for people on low and middle incomes . This raises revenue from a bad activity ( the creation of carbon pollution ) to fund good activity ( progressive reform of the taxation system ) . <p> This is a historic reform . It is clever policy . It is good policy . It 's a bit like spinning gold from straw . It 's exactly the kind of smart and gutsy approach we want to see from this Government , and from every government . <p> It also creates @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Update found that combining a carbon price with this sort of targeted tax reduction could promote employment and halve the short economic impacts of reducing emissions . This means Australia could achieve a minus 15 emissions target for around the same impact as the minus 5 target without these tax reductions . Clever , and good . <p> For those who like detail : the tax changes triple the tax free threshold from $6000 to $18,200 per year from July 2012 and rolls in most of the existing Low Income Tax Offset to make the personal tax system simpler and more transparent . This helps reduce barriers to employment by lowering the excessive effective marginal tax rates that create poverty traps for low income earners . <p> The most immediate benefit is that Australians moving from welfare to work will no longer have to wait a year or more for their tax refund , and instead will receive that money in every pay packet . <p> This is a significant first step towards the income tax reforms recommended in the Henry Review , although more needs to be done . ( @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ yet . ) <p> A final dimension is the comparison between this package and the Carbon Pollution Reduction Scheme that failed to attract the support required before the last election . Usually , you would expect a controversial package to return in a weaker , watered down form . In this case , minority government and good negotiation skills seems to have delivered the opposite . <p> The new package has a stronger long term target ( an 80 per cent reduction from 2000 levels by 2050 ) , more transparent and independent advice ( through the Climate Change Authority ) , a market based buy out of high polluting electricity generation , and more attention to complementary action on energy efficiency and land-based emissions reductions . <p> All in a context where only one side of the Parliament , and the cross benches , seem to have any interest in constructive debate about how to respond to the real and pressing challenges facing our nation . <p> The Government , the Greens , and the independent members of the MPCCC all deserve commendation for delivering leadership and a practical package @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ package positions Australia for the future . <p> Lin Hatfield Dodds is national director of UnitingCare Australia and chair of the Australia Institute . <h> Comments <p> Comments should be short , respectful and on topic . @@4224219 <h> Carbon tax hit list : naming Australia 's biggest polluters <p> The release on Tuesday of the Gillard government 's breakdown of the 500 firms forced to pay up under the carbon tax seemed -- on the surface -- reasonably comprehensive . <p> Climate Change Minister Greg Combet informed the nation that power stations , mines and heavy industry would be forced to cough up , as would a range of state government authorities . A total of 135 operated in NSW or the ACT , 110 were in Queensland , 85 in Victoria , 75 in Western Australia , 25 in South Australia , 20 in Tasmania and " fewer than 10 " in the Northern Territory . A further 45 were interstate operations . <p> But who are they ? Combet declined to name and shame the perpetrators , suggesting instead that their public airing breached a " confidentiality " provision of the Howard-era National Greenhouse and Energy Reporting Act . Which firms , reporters begged to know , are in the prime minister 's sights ? <p> A quick check of the last available @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . This list , published earlier this year and based on emissions from 2009-10 , details the 300 companies that emitted over 87,500 tonnes of CO2 equivalent -- both on their own sites and through secondary power and energy consumption . It reads like a who 's who of Australia 's top corporates poised to write out fat cheques to the government . <p> Unsurprisingly , Macquarie Generation tops the list at a massive 23.4 million tonnes emitted on its own premises , with Delta Electricity coming in next at 20.4 million . Steelmakers like Bluescope -- which will receive $180 million in compensation -- are on the next tier , followed by the big miners BHP Billiton , Rio Tinto and Anglo American at around 5 to 7 million . Qantas -- which has said it will hike fares to soak up its liability -- and Virgin Blue also get a guernsey at 4 million and 1.7 million tonnes respectively . <p> Keen readers will have noticed there are only 234 companies on the list that exceed the carbon tax 's 25,000 tonne limit for own-premises emissions . For some @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ year . So where are the additional 266 firms alluded to by Combet that round out the 500 ? <p> The NGER list is limited in two respects . <p> Under the carbon tax , we know companies that produce over 25,000 tonnes of emissions a year off their own bat must stump up $23 a tonne -- which works out to a minimum of $575,000 before free permits and compensation . But there is an important caveat -- the company must produce over 25,000 tonnes per site , which means those that exceed the 25k benchmark but whose operations are spread around the city or country will escape scrutiny . <p> A number of those on the NGER -- like Mars , McCain and Harvey Norman -- would probably be exempt because they operate multiple factories and stores . <p> Secondly , the list is divided into " Scope 1 " and " Scope 2 " emissions . Scope 1 relates to in house emissions while Scope 2 relates to energy used on site , but generated elsewhere . <p> Under the carbon tax , only Scope 1 emissions are @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ produce more than 87,500 tonnes of emissions in total -- regardless of whether they 're incurred under Scope 1 or Scope 2 . Therefore , there would be numerous -- perhaps 50 or so -- firms that exceed the carbon tax threshold but do not trigger the 87.5k total required for inclusion on the list . <p> A further hint to the identities of the missing 266 can be found in this line from Combet 's release : " The remaining 190 operate in the waste disposal sector . " Tellingly , the NGER list is suspiciously free of landfill , garbage and recycling firms , aside from behemoths like Transpacific Industries . The remainder would seem to be council tips which fall under the umbrella of local government . <p> In well under 12 months , and assuming those firms do n't out themselves in the meantime or get fingered by the government , the public will finally have a better picture . The 2010-11 version of the NGER , due out next year , will ratchet down to include all companies that emit 50,000 tonnes or more -- which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ tax 's scope . 
@@4224229 <h> How the carbon tax made me a Liberal <p> For a while now , I 've been increasingly convinced of the need to join a political party in order to really participate in our political system . I was torn , though : my economic moderation and belief in limited progressive taxation seemed more in line with the Liberal Party , while my liberal stance on many social and environmental issues seemed more at home with the Labor Party . <p> But with the announcement of the Carbon tax , the decision was made easy . The Labor Party has pretty clearly demonstrated its priority is on fundamentally reshaping the redistributive nature of the Australian tax system , and that it is willing to jeopardise something as important as pricing carbon in order to do so . <p> While the Labor Party had been quite open with its plans to compensate low-income earners who would be disadvantaged under the scheme , it has not been quite so open with its plans to substantially overcompensate a large number of Australians . It 's not just a slight overcompensation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ according to the government 's own publication , some households would be compensated in excess of 400 per cent of the anticipated costs . This clearly goes beyond the scope of the Carbon tax and into more wholesale economic reform , and it is economic reform that reflects the core beliefs of the Labor Party . <p> If Climate Change and implementing a price on carbon is as important as the Labor Party says it is - which is something I absolutely believe - then our representatives should be able to vote on it without also having to cast their lot in favour of significant new redistribution . <p> That is not to say redistribution is bad . All government is redistributive , by its very nature , and progressive taxation is a good thing . But the nature and degree of that redistribution is at the heart of our political debate : how much money should the government tax , from whom should it tax it , how should it tax it , and how should that money be spent . That is a conversation that should happen openly , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ about pricing consumption . <p> These are not minor changes . The reforms will take one million Australians out of the income tax system , and will compensate some citizens well in excess of the anticipated cost of the program . An individual Senior on $30k a year will incur an anticipated additional $281 in costs , but get $1,000 in assistance . A single income couple with three dependents and a taxable income of $35,000 a year will get $1,017 in benefits , but should anticipate $480 in additional costs . Meanwhile , an individual with an income of $75,000 a year will receive $140 in benefits to $417 in additional costs . Perhaps most surprisingly , a senior couple with a taxable income of $80,000 , split between the two of them at a 70:30 split , will receive $2,289 in additional benefits to $501 in anticipated additional costs . <p> How will these redistributions help curb consumption ? How are these redistributions in any way designed to address the true cost of carbon ? This is not a policy that simply seeks to compensate vulnerable individuals for the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ policy that is designed to redistribute wealth . <p> Selling redistribution under the guise of environmentalism is disingenuous , to say the least . In failing to provide a carbon tax bill free of a clearly redistributive economic agenda that fundamentally and substantially shifts the tax burden , the Labor Party has shown where its agenda really is , and why it is not a party of moderation . <p> A vote for the carbon tax is a vote for greater redistribution . And while that may please the hard left , it has sent this moderate running to the Liberal Party . <p> Erin Riley is a professional communicator , semi-professional academic , and amateur blogger.

@@4226072 <h> Why economists prefer a market-based response to climate change <p> The leader of the Opposition , Tony Abbott , is not easily embarrassed , but he showed a tiny bit of embarrassment when he had to concede that he could not find a single reputable economist to back his proposal for a ' direct action ' scheme to reduce CO2 emissions . <p> By contrast , large majorities back one or other of Abbott 's numerous previous positions on the issue , supporting a carbon tax , an emissions trading scheme , or some hybrid of the two . <p> An informal poll held at the Australian Conference of Economists found that only 11 per cent of attendees supported ' direct action ' , while 80 per cent favoured price-based mechanisms . From my discussions in the profession , I would say that the 11 per cent is an overstatement , and that those who responded this way fall into two groups : <p> Non-economists attending the conference on behalf of business organisations and other bodies tied to the conservative side of politics . <p> The small @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and correctly judge that , whatever his current statements , Abbott has no intention of doing anything about it , let alone a wasteful and costly program of direct action . <p> But why are economists so overwhelming in their support of market-based responses to climate change ? Of course , economists support markets in general , but the great majority of the profession also supports direct government intervention in appropriate cases . The overwhelming support for a market-based response to climate change reflects economists ' understanding of the nature of the problem . <p> Energy is essential to all aspects of modern life . On the other hand , our current methods of producing energy generate quantities of carbon dioxide too great for the atmosphere to absorb without producing a substantial disruption of the global climate . <p> So , we need to find a way to maintain the essential uses of energy while reducing carbon dioxide emissions . This can be done by using alternative energy technologies , by using energy more efficiently or by changing our consumption patterns to focus less on energy-intensive items ( such as lighting @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ items that do not require so much energy ( such as health services and telecommunications ) . <p> This problem raises a vast number of possible options , and the problem is to choose which will achieve the necessary reductions in emissions with the least possible disruption and economic cost . This is a difficult problem . For example , in reducing emissions from transport , the alternatives include the development of more fuel efficient private cars , expansion of public transport or the use of telecommunications ( phone calls , email , Skype and so on ) as a replacement for face-to-face meetings . It is not immediately obvious what mixture of these options is best , and there may well be other possibilities . <p> One solution is for government to appoint experts to identify the best methods of reducing emissions and then introduce regulations or other forms of ' direct action ' to ensure that these methods are adopted . Sometimes , this is a sensible solution . Most of us are not very good at comparing the purchase price of household goods , from light bulbs to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . So , it makes sense for government to impose energy efficiency requirements , such as the phasing out of incandescent light bulbs . <p> But in most cases , no body of government experts has the information needed to make the necessary trade-offs . The alternative solution is to make those responsible for carbon emissions pay a price , just as they do for goods and services of all kinds . <p> To see how this works , consider the Federal Government 's proposal for a price of $23/tonne . This will affect the decisions of businesses and households at a number of levels . First , consider electricity generators . They currently receive an average of about $40 for a megawatt-hour ( MWh ) of electricity ( about 4c a kWh ) . Generating that MWh from brown coal emits about 1.3 tonnes of CO2 , so a brown coal generator would have to pay around $30 MWh as the price of the carbon they emit . The corresponding figures are around $23/tonne for black coal , $10-15 tonne for gas and zero for most alternatives . <p> At @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is no longer profitable , and existing brown coal stations are likely to close earlier than they would otherwise ( the Government is planning to help this along with some adjustment assistance ) . Where supplies are available , gas-fired electricity is usually the cheapest option followed by black coal and wind . As prices rise and the costs of relatively new renewable technologies fall , they will become competitive , even without regulatory measures such as the renewable energy target . <p> Given that the costs of coal-fired and gas-fired power will rise , the price of electricity will also rise , probably by around $23 MW/h or 2.3c a kWh . That 's not huge , but it 's enough to provide businesses in particular with a stronger incentive to invest in more energy-efficient technologies . However , rather than governments specifying what investments companies should make , the imposition of a price lets them pick the option that is most cost-effective for their own business . <p> Finally , we can expect some changes in household consumption patterns . At the current carbon price these will be modest @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that are already taking place , such as a reduction in the use of private cars , particularly among the young . <p> It is sometimes argued that , if the Government compensates households for the increase in prices associated with a carbon tax , the incentive effects of the tax would be cancelled out . That 's an error ( except if the compensation is conditional on continuing to use energy ) . <p> To see what 's wrong with this , think about the price of bananas , which has risen to around $12/kg in the wake of cyclones and floods . Over the same period , most households have experienced wage increases that more than offset the higher price of bananas . But of course that does n't mean we keep on buying the same amount of bananas . Rather we switch to , say , apples , and use the money saved to buy other goods and services . <p> In thinking about the impact of the carbon tax on households , it 's worth doing some basic arithmetic . The total revenue raised by the tax @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ than 1 per cent of national income , and most households will get a fair bit of that back in lower income taxes or higher pensions . So , the net impact , even for households that are net losers is unlikely to be more than 0.5 per cent of income . For the median Australian household , that 's around $7 a week , which is a lot less than the variation that arises from all kinds of other sources . <p> It follows that , although the carbon tax will have a significant impact on our aggregate emissions of CO2 , mainly through its impact on electricity generation and energy use by business , the average household will barely notice it . This was true of the GST , which takes about $40 billion a year in revenue , and it will be just as true of the carbon tax at a quarter the size . <p> John Quiggin is an ARC Federation Fellow in Economics and Political Science at the University of Queensland .
